Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your feelings of restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… it is bothersome, definitely. Maybe the restlessness feels louder, but the detail mistakes cause problems too.\n\nLike I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet, or I’ll be doing our household budget, and I really think I’ve checked it. Then later I’ll realize I put in the wrong date, or I skipped a line, or I reversed numbers. And then I feel embarrassed because it looks careless, but I swear I was trying.\n\nThe budget one especially gets me worked up because then I start second-guessing everything, and my husband will be asking where a number came from, and I’m like, “I don’t know, I thought I entered it right.” So… yes, it’s bothersome, just maybe in a different way than the restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt like your restlessness and impulsivity were under control, and if so, how long did that last?\n\nPatient: I mean, I guess there were times it felt more manageable, but not really lately. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and all our routines kind of merged together… that was about a year and five months ago.\n\nBefore that I had my own way of doing things, even if it was a little messy, and now there are just more shared plans and decisions and… I don’t know, it’s like I can’t keep myself steady in the middle of it. So if it was under control, it hasn’t felt that way for maybe that whole year and a half-ish.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. And with volunteering too — like, I used to want to be the person who helped organize things, and then I’d say yes, and then somehow a piece of it would just… fall through. Not because I don’t care. I care a lot, probably too much, and then I feel terrible afterward.\n\nSo I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities, which is embarrassing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t, like, lost a job exactly — I’m not working right now — and I don’t think I’ve had a relationship completely end because of it. But it has definitely hurt things.

The fallout is more like… people at home and in my volunteer circles can’t always count on me the way they should be able to. I’ll mean to follow through, and I’ll even start out really motivated, and then some part gets missed or I drop the ball. And then I’m apologizing again, or trying to explain, and it just sounds like excuses.

So I’ve had to step back from responsibilities, especially volunteering, because I kept letting things fall through even when I was trying really hard. That’s been hard, because I like being helpful, and then I end up feeling like I’m causing more stress.

  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is I feel like I can’t really settle in my own body anymore. Like when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork, or even go over household plans with someone, I get so restless it feels almost impossible to stay with it.\n\nAnd it’s funny, I used to think it was just that I hated paperwork—because honestly, who likes it—but it’s more than that now. I’ll be sitting there and I just feel like I need to move or do anything else, and then the whole thing becomes this big stressful mess.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this kind of restlessness?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, if I look back, it was there when I was pretty little. Like elementary school little. I wasn’t thinking of it as “restlessness” then, of course—I just remember always being the kid with the disaster desk, papers shoved everywhere, and my mom finding permission slips crumpled up in my bag after they were already due.\n\nAnd teachers would write those notes, you know, like I talked when I wasn’t supposed to, or I couldn’t stay in my seat. I used to think they just didn’t like chatty kids, but… yeah, looking back, that feeling of not being able to just sit and be still was kind of already part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. And it’s not even always because I’m rushing, which is what people assume. Like I’ll make a volunteer sign-up sheet, or sit down with our household budget, and I really think I’m being careful—I’ll even look it over—and then later I’ll notice I put the wrong date, or skipped an entire line, or flipped numbers around.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it makes me feel kind of incompetent, honestly. And then I start second-guessing everything, like, “Did I check that? Did I actually check that?” And then somehow I end up reorganizing the kitchen drawer instead of fixing the spreadsheet, which… sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, careless mistakes happen a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I really don’t mean to, and I actually care a lot about being respectful, so it feels awful when I catch myself doing it.\n\nLike with my husband, if he’s telling a story or explaining something, I’ll jump in because I already think I know where he’s going. Or I’ll finish the sentence for him, not in a rude way—well, I guess it can feel rude to him—but in my head the answer is just there and it comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n\nAnd then I’m apologizing, and sometimes I’m like, “Okay, I’m going to just listen,” but then two minutes later I’ve done it again. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like blurting out answers or grabbing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so—especially with talking. I really do care about being respectful, like I’m not trying to steamroll people, but the response just comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n\nWith my husband especially, I’ll jump into what he’s saying or finish his thought because in my head I’m already at the end of the sentence. And then I realize I cut him off, and I feel bad. It’s not like I sat there and decided, “I’m going to interrupt now,” it just sort of happens. Then I’m apologizing and trying to be better, and—sorry, this reminds me of when we were trying to plan something and I kept jumping in with logistics before he even finished explaining what he meant. So yes, that kind of impulsive blurting, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Conversations are hard because I can feel myself sort of… waiting physically, if that makes sense. Like in a committee meeting, if I know I have something to say, I get tense and fidgety sitting there until it’s actually my turn. I’ll be watching for a gap, and then I start checking the time over and over, even though that doesn’t help anything.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling as waiting in line at the pharmacy—standing there, trying to be normal, but inside I’m like, “Can we just move?” And then I feel silly because it’s not an emergency, but my body reacts like I need to do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “organizing tasks” could mean a lot of things, right? Like organizing a closet versus organizing a whole day are very different—actually, I did try to reorganize our pantry once and ended up buying all these little containers, which looked great for about a week and then somehow everything was just… back to being shoved in there.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess I’d say I do notice it sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it “often” without knowing what you mean exactly. Like, are you asking about planning steps, keeping track of responsibilities, or physically keeping things organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m sitting still for very long, like in church meetings or at a family dinner, I’m usually doing something with my hands or feet without even realizing it at first.\n\nI’ll twist my wedding ring around and around, or bounce my foot under the table, or keep shifting in the chair like I can’t quite get settled. And I try to look composed, especially in church because you don’t want to seem distracting—well, and family dinners too, depending on who’s there—but my body is still kind of moving the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying focused in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot, and also in the volunteer things I’m involved in. I’m not working right now, so I can’t really speak to a workplace exactly, but in the places where people do depend on me, it’s become a problem.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to follow through, I really will, and then something slips or I don’t keep up with what I said I’d do. And then people can’t quite count on me, which feels awful. I’ve actually had to step back from some responsibilities in my volunteer circles because I kept letting things fall through even though I was trying hard. At home too, it creates this feeling like I’m not reliable, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with focus consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. Looking back, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid with the messy desk, the crumpled-up permission slips I forgot to give my parents—actually my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d find papers at the bottom of my backpack after the event had already happened.\n\nAnd teachers would send notes home saying I was talking when I shouldn’t, or that I couldn’t seem to stay in my seat. I mean, I didn’t think of it as anything back then, I was just… me. But when I look back now, it does seem like those same patterns were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I don’t always notice it in the moment, because I’m usually thinking, “Oh, I’ll get to that,” and then suddenly it’s three days later and someone’s asking me about the thing I said I would do.\n\nThe biggest fallout has been at home and in my volunteer circles. People can’t really count on me the way I want them to be able to, and that feels terrible. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I kept letting things fall through, even though I really was trying hard. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I care about those people and those commitments.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Like if my husband asks me to do a few things in a row—call the plumber, put something on the calendar, and send a form somewhere—I’ll start out okay and do the first thing, and then somehow I’m… I don’t know, I’m in the kitchen wiping the counter or checking something else, and the rest just doesn’t get finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not that I’m refusing or I don’t care. I really do mean to finish the whole list, but once I get pulled off track, it’s like the other steps just fall out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but what really made me come in is this restless feeling. Like I can’t settle in my own body anymore, especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or go over household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, you know, the bills or the calendar, or who’s driving where, and I get this urge to get up or do something else. Sorry, I know that’s not exactly choosing one. But the restlessness is the part that feels the loudest lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more problematic for you than your difficulty with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. The attention stuff is a problem too, but the thing that really pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or talk through household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just focus,” but my body feels like it’s trying to leave the room before I’ve even finished the conversation. Sorry, that’s probably not a neat answer. But yes, the restlessness feels more urgent right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your feelings of restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… it is bothersome, definitely. Maybe the restlessness feels louder, but the detail mistakes cause problems too.\n\nLike I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet, or I’ll be doing our household budget, and I really think I’ve checked it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I care about those people and those commitments.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Like if my husband asks me to do a few things in a row—call the plumber, put something on the calendar, and send a form somewhere—I’ll start out okay and do the first thing, and then somehow I’m… I don’t know, I’m in the kitchen wiping the counter or checking something else, and the rest just doesn’t get finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not that I’m refusing or I don’t care. I really do mean to finish the whole list, but once I get pulled off track, it’s like the other steps just fall out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but what really made me come in is this restless feeling. Like I can’t settle in my own body anymore, especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or go over household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, you know, the bills or the calendar, or who’s driving where, and I get this urge to get up or do something else. Sorry, I know that’s not exactly choosing one. But the restlessness is the part that feels the loudest lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more problematic for you than your difficulty with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. The attention stuff is a problem too, but the thing that really pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or talk through household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just focus,” but my body feels like it’s trying to leave the room before I’ve even finished the conversation. Sorry, that’s probably not a neat answer. But yes, the restlessness feels more urgent right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your feelings of restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… it is bothersome, definitely. Maybe the restlessness feels louder, but the detail mistakes cause problems too.\n\nLike I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet, or I’ll be doing our household budget, and I really think I’ve checked it. Then later I’ll realize I put in the wrong date, or I skipped a line, or I reversed numbers. And then I feel embarrassed because it looks careless, but I swear I was trying.\n\nThe budget one especially gets me worked up because then I start second-guessing everything, and my husband will be asking where a number came from, and I’m like, “I don’t know, I thought I entered it right.” So… yes, it’s bothersome, just maybe in a different way than the restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt like your restlessness and impulsivity were under control, and if so, how long did that last?\n\nPatient: I mean, I guess there were times it felt more manageable, but not really lately. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and all our routines kind of merged together… that was about a year and five months ago.\n\nBefore that I had my own way of doing things, even if it was a little messy, and now there are just more shared plans and decisions and… I don’t know, it’s like I can’t keep myself steady in the middle of it. So if it was under control, it hasn’t felt that way for maybe that whole year and a half-ish.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt home, people can’t always count on me to follow through, even when I honestly mean to. And with volunteering too — like, I used to want to be the person who helped organize things, and then I’d say yes, and then somehow a piece of it would just… fall through. Not because I don’t care. I care a lot, probably too much, and then I feel terrible afterward.\n\nSo I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities, which is embarrassing. And then I start thinking about how other people must see me, like I’m flaky or unreliable, and that’s hard in relationships. It’s not just in my head; it affects other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to maintain a job or a relationship because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty with focus?\n\nPatient: I haven’t, like, lost a job exactly — I’m not working right now — and I don’t think I’ve had a relationship completely end because of it. But it has definitely hurt things.\n\nThe fallout is more like… people at home and in my volunteer circles can’t always count on me the way they should be able to. I’ll mean to follow through, and I’ll even start out really motivated, and then some part gets missed or I drop the ball. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is I feel like I can’t really settle in my own body anymore. Like when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork, or even go over household plans with someone, I get so restless it feels almost impossible to stay with it.\n\nAnd it’s funny, I used to think it was just that I hated paperwork—because honestly, who likes it—but it’s more than that now. I’ll be sitting there and I just feel like I need to move or do anything else, and then the whole thing becomes this big stressful mess.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this kind of restlessness?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, if I look back, it was there when I was pretty little. Like elementary school little. I wasn’t thinking of it as “restlessness” then, of course—I just remember always being the kid with the disaster desk, papers shoved everywhere, and my mom finding permission slips crumpled up in my bag after they were already due.\n\nAnd teachers would write those notes, you know, like I talked when I wasn’t supposed to, or I couldn’t stay in my seat. I used to think they just didn’t like chatty kids, but… yeah, looking back, that feeling of not being able to just sit and be still was kind of already part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. And it’s not even always because I’m rushing, which is what people assume. Like I’ll make a volunteer sign-up sheet, or sit down with our household budget, and I really think I’m being careful—I’ll even look it over—and then later I’ll notice I put the wrong date, or skipped an entire line, or flipped numbers around.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it makes me feel kind of incompetent, honestly. And then I start second-guessing everything, like, “Did I check that? Did I actually check that?” And then somehow I end up reorganizing the kitchen drawer instead of fixing the spreadsheet, which… sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, careless mistakes happen a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I really don’t mean to, and I actually care a lot about being respectful, so it feels awful when I catch myself doing it.\n\nLike with my husband, if he’s telling a story or explaining something, I’ll jump in because I already think I know where he’s going. Or I’ll finish the sentence for him, not in a rude way—well, I guess it can feel rude to him—but in my head the answer is just there and it comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n\nAnd then I’m apologizing, and sometimes I’m like, “Okay, I’m going to just listen,” but then two minutes later I’ve done it again. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like blurting out answers or grabbing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so—especially with talking. I really do care about being respectful, like I’m not trying to steamroll people, but the response just comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n\nWith my husband especially, I’ll jump into what he’s saying or finish his thought because in my head I’m already at the end of the sentence. And then I realize I cut him off, and I feel bad. It’s not like I sat there and decided, “I’m going to interrupt now,” it just sort of happens. Then I’m apologizing and trying to be better, and—sorry, this reminds me of when we were trying to plan something and I kept jumping in with logistics before he even finished explaining what he meant. So yes, that kind of impulsive blurting, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Conversations are hard because I can feel myself sort of… waiting physically, if that makes sense. Like in a committee meeting, if I know I have something to say, I get tense and fidgety sitting there until it’s actually my turn. I’ll be watching for a gap, and then I start checking the time over and over, even though that doesn’t help anything.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling as waiting in line at the pharmacy—standing there, trying to be normal, but inside I’m like, “Can we just move?” And then I feel silly because it’s not an emergency, but my body reacts like I need to do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “organizing tasks” could mean a lot of things, right? Like organizing a closet versus organizing a whole day are very different—actually, I did try to reorganize our pantry once and ended up buying all these little containers, which looked great for about a week and then somehow everything was just… back to being shoved in there.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess I’d say I do notice it sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it “often” without knowing what you mean exactly. Like, are you asking about planning steps, keeping track of responsibilities, or physically keeping things organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m sitting still for very long, like in church meetings or at a family dinner, I’m usually doing something with my hands or feet without even realizing it at first.\n\nI’ll twist my wedding ring around and around, or bounce my foot under the table, or keep shifting in the chair like I can’t quite get settled. And I try to look composed, especially in church because you don’t want to seem distracting—well, and family dinners too, depending on who’s there—but my body is still kind of moving the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying focused in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot, and also in the volunteer things I’m involved in. I’m not working right now, so I can’t really speak to a workplace exactly, but in the places where people do depend on me, it’s become a problem.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to follow through, I really will, and then something slips or I don’t keep up with what I said I’d do. And then people can’t quite count on me, which feels awful. I’ve actually had to step back from some responsibilities in my volunteer circles because I kept letting things fall through even though I was trying hard. At home too, it creates this feeling like I’m not reliable, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with focus consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. Looking back, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid with the messy desk, the crumpled-up permission slips I forgot to give my parents—actually my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d find papers at the bottom of my backpack after the event had already happened.\n\nAnd teachers would send notes home saying I was talking when I shouldn’t, or that I couldn’t seem to stay in my seat. I mean, I didn’t think of it as anything back then, I was just… me. But when I look back now, it does seem like those same patterns were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I don’t always notice it in the moment, because I’m usually thinking, “Oh, I’ll get to that,” and then suddenly it’s three days later and someone’s asking me about the thing I said I would do.\n\nThe biggest fallout has been at home and in my volunteer circles. People can’t really count on me the way I want them to be able to, and that feels terrible. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I kept letting things fall through, even though I really was trying hard. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I care about those people and those commitments.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Like if my husband asks me to do a few things in a row—call the plumber, put something on the calendar, and send a form somewhere—I’ll start out okay and do the first thing, and then somehow I’m… I don’t know, I’m in the kitchen wiping the counter or checking something else, and the rest just doesn’t get finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not that I’m refusing or I don’t care. I really do mean to finish the whole list, but once I get pulled off track, it’s like the other steps just fall out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but what really made me come in is this restless feeling. Like I can’t settle in my own body anymore, especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or go over household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, you know, the bills or the calendar, or who’s driving where, and I get this urge to get up or do something else. Sorry, I know that’s not exactly choosing one. But the restlessness is the part that feels the loudest lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more problematic for you than your difficulty with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. The attention stuff is a problem too, but the thing that really pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or talk through household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just focus,” but my body feels like it’s trying to leave the room before I’ve even finished the conversation. Sorry, that’s probably not a neat answer. But yes, the restlessness feels more urgent right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your feelings of restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… it is bothersome, definitely. Maybe the restlessness feels louder, but the detail mistakes cause problems too.\n\nLike I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet, or I’ll be doing our household budget, and I really think I’ve checked it. Then later I’ll realize I put in the wrong date, or I skipped a line, or I reversed numbers. And then I feel embarrassed because it looks careless, but I swear I was trying.\n\nThe budget one especially gets me worked up because then I start second-guessing everything, and my husband will be asking where a number came from, and I’m like, “I don’t know, I thought I entered it right.” So… yes, it’s bothersome, just maybe in a different way than the restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt like your restlessness and impulsivity were under control, and if so, how long did that last?\n\nPatient: I mean, I guess there were times it felt more manageable, but not really lately. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and all our routines kind of merged together… that was about a year and five months ago.\n\nBefore that I had my own way of doing things, even if it was a little messy, and now there are just more shared plans and decisions and… I don’t know, it’s like I can’t keep myself steady in the middle of it. So if it was under control, it hasn’t felt that way for maybe that whole year and a half-ish.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt home, people can’t always count on me to follow through, even when I honestly mean to. And with volunteering too — like, I used to want to be the person who helped organize things, and then I’d say yes, and then somehow a piece of it would just… fall through. Not because I don’t care. I care a lot, probably too much, and then I feel terrible afterward.\n\nSo I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities, which is embarrassing. And then I start thinking about how other people must see me, like I’m flaky or unreliable, and that’s hard in relationships. It’s not just in my head; it affects other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to maintain a job or a relationship because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty with focus?\n\nPatient: I haven’t, like, lost a job exactly — I’m not working right now — and I don’t think I’ve had a relationship completely end because of it. But it has definitely hurt things.\n\nThe fallout is more like… people at home and in my volunteer circles can’t always count on me the way they should be able to. I’ll mean to follow through, and I’ll even start out really motivated, and then some part gets missed or I drop the ball. And then I’m apologizing again, or trying to explain, and it just sounds like excuses.\n\nSo I’ve had to step back from responsibilities, especially volunteering, because I kept letting things fall through even when I was trying really hard. That’s been hard, because I like being helpful, and then I end up feeling like I’m causing more stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with focus in more than one setting, such as at home, in social situations, and in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out by setting, honestly. I notice it most at home because that’s where everything is… right in front of me, you know? Like the shared routines and expectations.\n\nSocially, I guess sometimes it comes up, but I don’t always know if that’s the same thing or just me being tired or distracted. And with other areas, like volunteering, there have been times I’ve struggled to stay on top of things, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nSorry, I feel like I’m circling around the question. I’ll be sitting there and I just feel like I need to move or do anything else, and then the whole thing becomes this big stressful mess.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this kind of restlessness?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, if I look back, it was there when I was pretty little. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it was there pretty early, yeah. Looking back, elementary school is where I can see it most clearly — I was always the kid with the disaster of a desk, like papers shoved everywhere, permission slips disappearing until the morning they were due.

And teachers would send home notes about me talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or getting up, or seeming like I just couldn’t stay in my seat. At the time I think everyone just thought I was kind of chatty or messy… I mean, my mom used to joke about my backpack being a black hole, which is funny now but also not really. So, no, I don’t think it just suddenly started as an adult.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is I feel like I can’t really settle in my own body anymore. Like when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork, or even go over household plans with someone, I get so restless it feels almost impossible to stay with it.\n\nAnd it’s funny, I used to think it was just that I hated paperwork—because honestly, who likes it—but it’s more than that now. I’ll be sitting there and I just feel like I need to move or do anything else, and then the whole thing becomes this big stressful mess.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this kind of restlessness?\n\nPatient: I think… honestly, if I look back, it was there when I was pretty little. Like elementary school little. I wasn’t thinking of it as “restlessness” then, of course—I just remember always being the kid with the disaster desk, papers shoved everywhere, and my mom finding permission slips crumpled up in my bag after they were already due.\n\nAnd teachers would write those notes, you know, like I talked when I wasn’t supposed to, or I couldn’t stay in my seat. I used to think they just didn’t like chatty kids, but… yeah, looking back, that feeling of not being able to just sit and be still was kind of already part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. And it’s not even always because I’m rushing, which is what people assume. Like I’ll make a volunteer sign-up sheet, or sit down with our household budget, and I really think I’m being careful—I’ll even look it over—and then later I’ll notice I put the wrong date, or skipped an entire line, or flipped numbers around.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it makes me feel kind of incompetent, honestly. And then I start second-guessing everything, like, “Did I check that? Did I actually check that?” And then somehow I end up reorganizing the kitchen drawer instead of fixing the spreadsheet, which… sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, careless mistakes happen a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I really don’t mean to, and I actually care a lot about being respectful, so it feels awful when I catch myself doing it.\n\nLike with my husband, if he’s telling a story or explaining something, I’ll jump in because I already think I know where he’s going. Or I’ll finish the sentence for him, not in a rude way—well, I guess it can feel rude to him—but in my head the answer is just there and it comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n\nAnd then I’m apologizing, and sometimes I’m like, “Okay, I’m going to just listen,” but then two minutes later I’ve done it again. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like blurting out answers or grabbing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so—especially with talking. I really do care about being respectful, like I’m not trying to steamroll people, but the response just comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n\nWith my husband especially, I’ll jump into what he’s saying or finish his thought because in my head I’m already at the end of the sentence. And then I realize I cut him off, and I feel bad. It’s not like I sat there and decided, “I’m going to interrupt now,” it just sort of happens. Then I’m apologizing and trying to be better, and—sorry, this reminds me of when we were trying to plan something and I kept jumping in with logistics before he even finished explaining what he meant. So yes, that kind of impulsive blurting, definitely.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Conversations are hard because I can feel myself sort of… waiting physically, if that makes sense. Like in a committee meeting, if I know I have something to say, I get tense and fidgety sitting there until it’s actually my turn. I’ll be watching for a gap, and then I start checking the time over and over, even though that doesn’t help anything.\n\nIt’s kind of the same feeling as waiting in line at the pharmacy—standing there, trying to be normal, but inside I’m like, “Can we just move?” And then I feel silly because it’s not an emergency, but my body reacts like I need to do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, “organizing tasks” could mean a lot of things, right? Like organizing a closet versus organizing a whole day are very different—actually, I did try to reorganize our pantry once and ended up buying all these little containers, which looked great for about a week and then somehow everything was just… back to being shoved in there.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess I’d say I do notice it sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it “often” without knowing what you mean exactly. Like, are you asking about planning steps, keeping track of responsibilities, or physically keeping things organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m sitting still for very long, like in church meetings or at a family dinner, I’m usually doing something with my hands or feet without even realizing it at first.\n\nI’ll twist my wedding ring around and around, or bounce my foot under the table, or keep shifting in the chair like I can’t quite get settled. And I try to look composed, especially in church because you don’t want to seem distracting—well, and family dinners too, depending on who’s there—but my body is still kind of moving the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying focused in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s not just one place. At home it shows up a lot, and also in the volunteer things I’m involved in. I’m not working right now, so I can’t really speak to a workplace exactly, but in the places where people do depend on me, it’s become a problem.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to follow through, I really will, and then something slips or I don’t keep up with what I said I’d do. And then people can’t quite count on me, which feels awful. I’ve actually had to step back from some responsibilities in my volunteer circles because I kept letting things fall through even though I was trying hard. At home too, it creates this feeling like I’m not reliable, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with focus consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. Looking back, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid with the messy desk, the crumpled-up permission slips I forgot to give my parents—actually my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d find papers at the bottom of my backpack after the event had already happened.\n\nAnd teachers would send notes home saying I was talking when I shouldn’t, or that I couldn’t seem to stay in my seat. I mean, I didn’t think of it as anything back then, I was just… me. But when I look back now, it does seem like those same patterns were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I don’t always notice it in the moment, because I’m usually thinking, “Oh, I’ll get to that,” and then suddenly it’s three days later and someone’s asking me about the thing I said I would do.\n\nThe biggest fallout has been at home and in my volunteer circles. People can’t really count on me the way I want them to be able to, and that feels terrible. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I kept letting things fall through, even though I really was trying hard. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I care about those people and those commitments.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Like if my husband asks me to do a few things in a row—call the plumber, put something on the calendar, and send a form somewhere—I’ll start out okay and do the first thing, and then somehow I’m… I don’t know, I’m in the kitchen wiping the counter or checking something else, and the rest just doesn’t get finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not that I’m refusing or I don’t care. I really do mean to finish the whole list, but once I get pulled off track, it’s like the other steps just fall out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but what really made me come in is this restless feeling. Like I can’t settle in my own body anymore, especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or go over household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, you know, the bills or the calendar, or who’s driving where, and I get this urge to get up or do something else. Sorry, I know that’s not exactly choosing one. But the restlessness is the part that feels the loudest lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more problematic for you than your difficulty with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. The attention stuff is a problem too, but the thing that really pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or talk through household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just focus,” but my body feels like it’s trying to leave the room before I’ve even finished the conversation. Sorry, that’s probably not a neat answer. But yes, the restlessness feels more urgent right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your feelings of restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… it is bothersome, definitely. Maybe the restlessness feels louder, but the detail mistakes cause problems too.\n\nLike I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet, or I’ll be doing our household budget, and I really think I’ve checked it. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and all our routines kind of merged together… that was about a year and five months ago.\n\nBefore that I had my own way of doing things, even if it was a little messy, and now there are just more shared plans and decisions and… I don’t know, it’s like I can’t keep myself steady in the middle of it. At home it shows up a lot, and also in the volunteer things I’m involved in. I’m not working right now, so I can’t really speak to a workplace exactly, but in the places where people do depend on me, it’s become a problem.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to follow through, I really will, and then something slips or I don’t keep up with what I said I’d do. And then people can’t quite count on me, which feels awful. I’ve actually had to step back from some responsibilities in my volunteer circles because I kept letting things fall through even though I was trying hard. At home too, it creates this feeling like I’m not reliable, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with focus consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were significantly better or worse?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. Looking back, even in elementary school I was kind of that kid with the messy desk, the crumpled-up permission slips I forgot to give my parents—actually my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d find papers at the bottom of my backpack after the event had already happened.\n\nAnd teachers would send notes home saying I was talking when I shouldn’t, or that I couldn’t seem to stay in my seat. I mean, I didn’t think of it as anything back then, I was just… me. But when I look back now, it does seem like those same patterns were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I don’t always notice it in the moment, because I’m usually thinking, “Oh, I’ll get to that,” and then suddenly it’s three days later and someone’s asking me about the thing I said I would do.\n\nThe biggest fallout has been at home and in my volunteer circles. People can’t really count on me the way I want them to be able to, and that feels terrible. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I kept letting things fall through, even though I really was trying hard. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I care about those people and those commitments.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Like if my husband asks me to do a few things in a row—call the plumber, put something on the calendar, and send a form somewhere—I’ll start out okay and do the first thing, and then somehow I’m… I don’t know, I’m in the kitchen wiping the counter or checking something else, and the rest just doesn’t get finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not that I’m refusing or I don’t care. I really do mean to finish the whole list, but once I get pulled off track, it’s like the other steps just fall out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with paying attention to details and following through on tasks, or is it more with feeling restless and impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but what really made me come in is this restless feeling. Like I can’t settle in my own body anymore, especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or go over household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, you know, the bills or the calendar, or who’s driving where, and I get this urge to get up or do something else. Sorry, I know that’s not exactly choosing one. But the restlessness is the part that feels the loudest lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and impulsivity are more problematic for you than your difficulty with paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. The attention stuff is a problem too, but the thing that really pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Especially when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or talk through household plans.\n\nAnd then I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just focus,” but my body feels like it’s trying to leave the room before I’ve even finished the conversation. Sorry, that’s probably not a neat answer. But yes, the restlessness feels more urgent right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with paying attention to details is as bothersome to you as your feelings of restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… it is bothersome, definitely. Maybe the restlessness feels louder, but the detail mistakes cause problems too.\n\nLike I’ll put together a volunteer sign-up sheet, or I’ll be doing our household budget, and I really think I’ve checked it. Then later I’ll realize I put in the wrong date, or I skipped a line, or I reversed numbers. And then I feel embarrassed because it looks careless, but I swear I was trying.\n\nThe budget one especially gets me worked up because then I start second-guessing everything, and my husband will be asking where a number came from, and I’m like, “I don’t know, I thought I entered it right.” So… yes, it’s bothersome, just maybe in a different way than the restless feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt like your restlessness and impulsivity were under control, and if so, how long did that last?\n\nPatient: I mean, I guess there were times it felt more manageable, but not really lately. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and all our routines kind of merged together… that was about a year and five months ago.\n\nBefore that I had my own way of doing things, even if it was a little messy, and now there are just more shared plans and decisions and… I don’t know, it’s like I can’t keep myself steady in the middle of it. So if it was under control, it hasn’t felt that way for maybe that whole year and a half-ish.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt home, people can’t always count on me to follow through, even when I honestly mean to. And with volunteering too — like, I used to want to be the person who helped organize things, and then I’d say yes, and then somehow a piece of it would just… fall through. Not because I don’t care. I care a lot, probably too much, and then I feel terrible afterward.\n\nSo I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities, which is embarrassing. And then I start thinking about how other people must see me, like I’m flaky or unreliable, and that’s hard in relationships. It’s not just in my head; it affects other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to maintain a job or a relationship because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty with focus?\n\nPatient: I haven’t, like, lost a job exactly — I’m not working right now — and I don’t think I’ve had a relationship completely end because of it. But it has definitely hurt things.\n\nThe fallout is more like… people at home and in my volunteer circles can’t always count on me the way they should be able to. I’ll mean to follow through, and I’ll even start out really motivated, and then some part gets missed or I drop the ball. And then I’m apologizing again, or trying to explain, and it just sounds like excuses.\n\nSo I’ve had to step back from responsibilities, especially volunteering, because I kept letting things fall through even when I was trying really hard. That’s been hard, because I like being helpful, and then I end up feeling like I’m causing more stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty with focus in more than one setting, such as at home, in social situations, and in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out by setting, honestly. I notice it most at home because that’s where everything is… right in front of me, you know? Like the shared routines and expectations.\n\nSocially, I guess sometimes it comes up, but I don’t always know if that’s the same thing or just me being tired or distracted. And with other areas, like volunteering, there have been times I’ve struggled to stay on top of things, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nSorry, I feel like I’m circling around the question. I think it’s not just one place, but I notice it more in some situations than others.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have been present in some form since childhood, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was there pretty early, yeah. Looking back, elementary school is where I can see it most clearly — I was always the kid with the disaster of a desk, like papers shoved everywhere, permission slips disappearing until the morning they were due.\n\nAnd teachers would send home notes about me talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or getting up, or seeming like I just couldn’t stay in my seat. At the time I think everyone just thought I was kind of chatty or messy… I mean, my mom used to joke about my backpack being a black hole, which is funny now but also not really. So, no, I don’t think it just suddenly started as an adult."
